[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

- You have exactly one tool: `python`.

Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. - Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

Now let me compare the container segments with the top box patterns. Let me match them up.

[TOOL CALL: python]
<tool_call>
<function=python>
<parameter=code>
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

# Top box 5x5 interior patterns
boxes = [
    (4, 12, 'S', 'S1'), (4, 22, 'P', 'P1'), (4, 35, 'S', 'S2'), (4, 45, 'P', 'P2'),
    (13, 12, 'S', 'S3'), (13, 22, 'P', 'P3'), (13, 35, 'S', 'S4'), (13, 45, 'P', 'P4'),
    (22, 12, 'S', 'S5'), (22, 22, 'P', 'P5'), (22, 35, 'S', 'S6'), (22, 45, 'P', 'P6'),
]

box_patterns = {}
for (r, c, color, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    box_patterns[label] = pat

# S container segments
s_segments = {}
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(41, 46))
    s_segments[f'Sseg{i}'] = seg

# P container segments
p_segments = {}
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(52, 57))
    p_segments[f'Pseg{i}'] = seg

# Match S segments to S boxes
print("S container segment matches:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in s_segments.items():
    for box_name, box_pat in box_patterns.items():
        if box_name.startswith('S') and seg_pat == box_pat:
            print(f"  {seg_name} matches {box_name}")

# Match P segments to P boxes
print("\nP container segment matches:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in p_segments.items():
    for box_name, box_pat in box_patterns.items():
        if box_name.startswith('P') and seg_pat == box_pat:
            print(f"  {seg_name} matches {box_name}")

# Check if any segments don't match
print("\nUnmatched S segments:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in s_segments.items():
    matched = any(seg_pat == box_patterns[bn] for bn in ['S1','S2','S3','S4','S5','S6'])
    if not matched:
        print(f"  {seg_name}")

print("\nUnmatched P segments:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in p_segments.items():
    matched = any(seg_pat == box_patterns[bn] for bn in ['P1','P2','P3','P4','P5','P6'])
    if not matched:
        print(f"  {seg_name}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

    n = len(grid)
    return [''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n)]

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)

ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

# Top box patterns
boxes = [
    (4, 12, 'S', 'S1'), (4, 22, 'P', 'P1'), (4, 35, 'S', 'S2'), (4, 45, 'P', 'P2'),
    (13, 12, 'S', 'S3'), (13, 22, 'P', 'P3'), (13, 35, 'S', 'S4'), (13, 45, 'P', 'P4'),
    (22, 12, 'S', 'S5'), (22, 22, 'P', 'P5'), (22, 35, 'S', 'S6'), (22, 45, 'P', 'P6'),
]

box_patterns = {}
for (r, c, color, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    box_patterns[label] = pat

# Current S container segments
s_segs = {}
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(41, 46))
    s_segs[f'Sseg{i}'] = seg

# Current P container segments
p_segs = {}
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(52, 57))
    p_segs[f'Pseg{i}'] = seg

# Check which top box each container segment matches (including rotations)
def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

print("S container segment matches:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in s_segs.items():
    matches = []
    for box_name, box_pat in box_patterns.items():
        if box_name.startswith('S'):
            for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(box_pat)):
                if rot == seg_pat:
                    matches.append(f"{box_name} rot{rot_idx}")
    print(f"  {seg_name}: {matches if matches else 'no match'}")

print("\nP container segment matches:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in p_segs.items():
    matches = []
    for box_name, box_pat in box_patterns.items():
        if box_name.startswith('P'):
            for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(box_pat)):
                if rot == seg_pat:
                    matches.append(f"{box_name} rot{rot_idx}")
    print(f"  {seg_name}: {matches if matches else 'no match'}")

ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

# P3 pattern (from top box at 13,22)
p3 = tuple(rows[13+i][22+1:22+6] for i in range(1, 6))
print("P3:", list(p3))

# Pseg3 current
pseg3 = tuple(rows[r][14+3*7+1:14+3*7+6] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg3:", list(pseg3))

# Check if Pseg3 is a rotation of P3
def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

print("P3 rot90:", list(rotate_cw(p3)))
print("P3 rot180:", list(rotate_cw(rotate_cw(p3))))
print("P3 rot270:", list(rotate_ccw(p3)))
print()
print("Pseg3 == P3 rot0?", pseg3 == p3)
print("Pseg3 == P3 rot90?", pseg3 == rotate_cw(p3))
print("Pseg3 == P3 rot180?", pseg3 == rotate_cw(rotate_cw(p3)))
print("Pseg3 == P3 rot270?", pseg3 == rotate_ccw(p3))

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.

- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

# Top P boxes
boxes = [
    (4, 22, 'P1'), (4, 45, 'P2'),
    (13, 22, 'P3'), (13, 45, 'P4'),

p_patterns = {}
for (r, c, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    p_patterns[label] = pat

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

# Current Pseg4

<parameter=code>
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

# Top box patterns
boxes = [
    (4, 12, 'S', 'S1'), (4, 22, 'P', 'P1'), (4, 35, 'S', 'S2'), (4, 45, 'P', 'P2'),
    (13, 12, 'S', 'S3'), (13, 22, 'P', 'P3'), (13, 35, 'S', 'S4'), (13, 45, 'P', 'P4'),
    (22, 12, 'S', 'S5'), (22, 22, 'P', 'P5'), (22, 35, 'S', 'S6'), (22, 45, 'P', 'P6'),
]

box_patterns = {}
for (r, c, color, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    box_patterns[label] = pat

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

# Current S container segments
s_segs = {}
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(41, 46))
    s_segs[f'Sseg{i}'] = seg

# Current P container segments
p_segs = {}
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(52, 57))
    p_segs[f'Pseg{i}'] = seg

print("S container segment matches:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in s_segs.items():
    matches = []
    for box_name, box_pat in box_patterns.items():
        if box_name.startswith('S'):
            for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(box_pat)):
                if rot == seg_pat:
                    matches.append(f"{box_name} rot{rot_idx}")
    print(f"  {seg_name}: {matches if matches else 'no match'}")

print("\nP container segment matches:")
for seg_name, seg_pat in p_segs.items():
    matches = []
    for box_name, box_pat in box_patterns.items():
        if box_name.startswith('P'):
            for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(box_pat)):
                if rot == seg_pat:
                    matches.append(f"{box_name} rot{rot_idx}")
    print(f"  {seg_name}: {matches if matches else 'no match'}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

# Check what pattern Pseg0 is now
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

pseg0 = tuple(rows[r][15:20] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg0 after DOWN:", list(pseg0))

# Top P boxes
boxes = [
    (4, 22, 'P1'), (4, 45, 'P2'),
    (13, 22, 'P3'), (13, 45, 'P4'),
    (22, 22, 'P5'), (22, 45, 'P6'),
]

p_patterns = {}
for (r, c, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    p_patterns[label] = pat

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, pat in p_patterns.items():
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(pat)):
        if rot == pseg0:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

<parameter=code>
# Check what pattern Pseg0 is now
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

pseg0 = tuple(rows[r][15:20] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg0 after DOWN#3:", list(pseg0))

# Top P boxes
boxes = [
    (4, 22, 'P1'), (4, 45, 'P2'),
    (13, 22, 'P3'), (13, 45, 'P4'),
    (22, 22, 'P5'), (22, 45, 'P6'),
]

p_patterns = {}
for (r, c, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    p_patterns[label] = pat

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, pat in p_patterns.items():
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(pat)):
        if rot == pseg0:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# So the sequence for Pseg0 with DOWN is:
# P3 rot90 -> P5 rot270 -> P5 rot270 -> P? 
# Wait, DOWN#2 gave P5 rot270, and DOWN#3 gave a new pattern.
# Let me check if DOWN cycles through patterns.

# Let me trace the full sequence:
# Initial: P3 rot90
# DOWN#1: P5 rot270
# UP#1: P3 rot90
# DOWN#2: P5 rot270
# DOWN#3: P? (current)

# So DOWN cycles: P3 rot90 -> P5 rot270 -> P? 
# And UP goes back: P? -> P5 rot270 -> P3 rot90

# So DOWN and UP are not just toggles. DOWN cycles forward, UP cycles backward.
print("\nDOWN sequence for Pseg0:")
print("  P3 rot90 -> P5 rot270 -> ? (current)")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

# Check Pseg0 after DOWN#4
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg0 = tuple(rows[r][15:20] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg0 after DOWN#4:", list(pseg0))

# Check what it matches
boxes = [
    (4, 22, 'P1'), (4, 45, 'P2'),
    (13, 22, 'P3'), (13, 45, 'P4'),
    (22, 22, 'P5'), (22, 45, 'P6'),
]

p_patterns = {}
for (r, c, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    p_patterns[label] = pat

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, pat in p_patterns.items():
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(pat)):
        if rot == pseg0:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# Full DOWN sequence for Pseg0:
# P3 rot90 -> P5 rot270 -> P4 -> ? 
print("\nDOWN sequence so far:")
print("  P3 rot90 -> P5 rot270 -> P4 -> ?")

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.

- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.

Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.

# Check Pseg0 after DOWN#6
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg0 = tuple(rows[r][15:20] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg0 after DOWN#6:", list(pseg0))

boxes = [
    (4, 22, 'P1'), (4, 45, 'P2'),
    (13, 22, 'P3'), (13, 45, 'P4'),
    (22, 22, 'P5'), (22, 45, 'P6'),
]

p_patterns = {}
for (r, c, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    p_patterns[label] = pat

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, pat in p_patterns.items():
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(pat)):
        if rot == pseg0:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# DOWN sequence for Pseg0:
# P3 rot90 -> P5 rot270 -> P4 -> P? -> P2 rot270 -> P?
print("\nDOWN sequence for Pseg0:")
print("  P3 rot90 -> P5 rot270 -> P4 -> P? -> P2 rot270 -> P?")

- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.

- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# Check Pseg0 after DOWN#8
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg0 = tuple(rows[r][15:20] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg0 after DOWN#8:", list(pseg0))

boxes = [
    (4, 22, 'P1'), (4, 45, 'P2'),
    (13, 22, 'P3'), (13, 45, 'P4'),
    (22, 22, 'P5'), (22, 45, 'P6'),
]

p_patterns = {}
for (r, c, label) in boxes:
    pat = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    p_patterns[label] = pat

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, pat in p_patterns.items():
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(pat)):
        if rot == pseg0:

ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

# Extract inner 5x5 of each P box
p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

unmatched = ('BBBBB', 'BPPPB', 'BBBBB', 'PBPBP', 'PBBBP')

print("Checking unmatched pattern against inner 5x5 of P boxes:")
for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == unmatched:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# Also check S boxes

    'S1': (4, 12), 'S2': (4, 35), 'S3': (13, 12),

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.

If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.

Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.

- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# Check Pseg1 after DOWN
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

# Pseg1 inner is at cols 22-26 (start=21, inner=22-26)
pseg1 = tuple(rows[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg1 after DOWN:", list(pseg1))

# Check what it matches
p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg1:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# Pseg1 was P6 initially, now after DOWN it should be next in cycle
# Let me check what the initial Pseg1 was
print("\nPseg1 initial: ['PBBBP', 'PBPBP', 'BBBBB', 'PBPBP', 'PBBBP']")
print("This should match P6")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

    n = len(grid)

Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

<parameter=code>
# Check Pseg1 after DOWN#3
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg1 = tuple(rows[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg1 after DOWN#3:", list(pseg1))

p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg1:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# Pseg1 DOWN cycle: P6 -> P3 rot0 -> P5 rot180 -> P?
print("\nPseg1 DOWN cycle: P6 -> P3 rot0 -> P5 rot180 -> P?")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

# Check Pseg1 after DOWN#4
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg1 = tuple(rows[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg1 after DOWN#4:", list(pseg1))

p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg1:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# Pseg1 DOWN cycle: P6 -> P3 rot0 -> P5 rot180 -> P4 -> P?

[TOOL CALL: python]
<tool_call>
<function=python>
<parameter=code>
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

current = ('BBBPP', 'BPBBB', 'BPBPB', 'BPBBB', 'BBBPP')

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == current:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

print("No match found for current Pseg1 pattern")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. - In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. <parameter=code>
# Check Pseg1 after DOWN#5
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg1 = tuple(rows[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg1 after DOWN#5:", list(pseg1))

p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg1:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# Pseg1 DOWN cycle so far: P6 -> P3 -> P5 rot180 -> P4 -> ? -> ?
print("\nPseg1 DOWN cycle: P6 -> P3 rot0 -> P5 rot180 -> P4 -> ? -> ?")

# Check Pseg1 after DOWN#6
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg1 = tuple(rows[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg1 after DOWN#6:", list(pseg1))

p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+i][c+1:c+6] for i in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg1:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')

p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

# Check each P segment
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(52, 57))
    matches = []
    for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
        inner = tuple(rows[r+j][c+1:c+6] for j in range(1, 6))
        for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
            if rot == seg:
                matches.append(f"{label} rot{rot_idx}")
    print(f"Pseg{i}: {list(seg)} -> {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

# Also check S container

- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.

<parameter=code>
# Check Pseg1 after 2 DOWNs
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg1 = tuple(rows[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg1 after 2 DOWNs:", list(pseg1))

# Check what it matches
p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+j][c+1:c+6] for j in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg1:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+j][c+1:c+6] for j in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg2:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

<parameter=code>
# Check Pseg2 after 4 total DOWNs from P5
ascii = current_frame.ascii
rows = ascii.split('\n')
pseg2 = tuple(rows[r][29:34] for r in range(52, 57))
print("Pseg2 current:", list(pseg2))

p_boxes = {
    'P1': (4, 22), 'P2': (4, 45), 'P3': (13, 22),
    'P4': (13, 45), 'P5': (22, 22), 'P6': (22, 45)
}

def rotate_cw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[n-1-j][i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def rotate_ccw(grid):
    n = len(grid)
    return tuple(''.join(grid[j][n-1-i] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))

def all_rotations(grid):
    return [grid, rotate_cw(grid), rotate_cw(rotate_cw(grid)), rotate_ccw(grid)]

for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
    inner = tuple(rows[r+j][c+1:c+6] for j in range(1, 6))
    for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
        if rot == pseg2:
            print(f"  Matches {label} rot{rot_idx}")

# Check all P segments
print("\nAll P segments:")
for i in range(5):
    start = 14 + i*7
    seg = tuple(rows[r][start+1:start+6] for r in range(52, 57))
    matches = []
    for label, (r, c) in p_boxes.items():
        inner = tuple(rows[r+j][c+1:c+6] for j in range(1, 6))
        for rot_idx, rot in enumerate(all_rotations(inner)):
            if rot == seg:
                matches.append(f"{label} rot{rot_idx}")
    print(f"  Pseg{i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>
